Gave the consent, refused to take the employer's head off the sheet, filled the *alternative considered* box on the gate form, brought Danner back twice, **put a sand and cement mix in a hole about eleven feet along in a fence about four feet high, on the Wednesday, because he was told to on the Friday and the man asked for it in writing** — and it is on the page that a man who had told a foreman to tell anybody who wanted to see the reach to bring them down had, on the same morning, had a hole in a fence filled, **and nobody caused it and nobody stopped it, and that is the story of the seventh of December.**
- **Petts** — still saying *nobody told us*, every working day, including the sixth of December in a test that passed and the reach that went out of true under him, and he is one of the eleven hurt, and nobody has asked him to stop and Milo Rook has not asked him to stop and has written down twice that he has not asked.
- **Five pumps** on three streets the works is not responsible for. **A third pump came off Bridge Street on the sixth of December at about eleven**, and the low streets went under nine inches by half past two against a district flood line of nine, and the gully was coming up on the low end for the thirty-ninth day in a row in a month with under an inch of rain. **The fire engine with a man volunteering on it for eleven weeks, paid out of a petty float, is in the file and was never told to anybody.**


### The works, the gang and the men in them — Volume 02 Batch 0001

- **The quay section gang: eleven men, Monday and Thursday at half past seven, the back length, a foreman and a weekly sheet that has six columns and no column for anybody's opinion, and a rack and a tin.** The foreman is **Roy Pryce, fifty-four, thirty-one years.** He walked the length a second time on the Monday the twenty-second, at 11.06, because a conductor's manifest was read down a telephone to him and a foreman understood a blank line at the bottom of it, **and he wrote his reason in the hours column in nine words and refused to write one word about thirty-one passengers, and he is right.** A man of about twenty-six on that gang stops in the down loop to look at a drain every Monday and Thursday, has done for two years, and **nobody in the district has ever asked him what he is looking at and Doreen Sankey is not going to start.** **The works did not shut over Christmas and the gang was on the length on the twenty-fourth and the twenty-eighth and both were written down.**
- **Cyril Sale, works clerk, forty-six, four years in the job, whose predecessor was a woman called Mrs Ivell for twenty-two years.** He sent the third edition of a maintenance schedule to the timetable division on the eighth of October because a man asked him for it *for index* on a telephone, and he has asked somebody to check whether he thought about the note he wrote in November. **He is the man who said the bridge sentence and who said there is no form, and he is not a villain and is not going to be one, and he asked for a week before he thinks of a way round it and it is not his to find.**
- **The permanent way superintendent's office exists in this batch only as a signatory's office** and nobody in it appears. Danner, Rennick, Petts and Verrick's spade are Volume 01 and are not in these ten chapters, and the flood-gate project, the culvert and the pinned reach are not touched by anything that happens here.

## Jo Lask

- **Where he is:** driving on the diversion. **Not suspended. His four weeks ran out on the twentieth of November and half pay stopped then. His hearing still has no date, and it has no date because an inquiry takes a man in a good coat four months and his has been in a queue since October.** He drove a car through the interchange at five minutes to twelve on the sixth of December, with nobody hurt, and got out of it and stood in the middle of it.
- **What he extracted, and what it cost him.** On the twenty-first of November he asked the tram authority for a written interval guarantee on the reach, on three terms, **in exchange for the extra Bellwether stop not being printed on the public timetable, the diversion panel carrying no name, and the district making no claim against the amendment of the first in any court, forum or review.** Granted on the third of December, printed, in a rule book, **naming him: *Conductor called out: J. LASK, conductor, Alder Quay depot*.** *I have said no to a man three times and yes to a rule book, and a man can be argued with and a rule book cannot, and that is why the rule book is better and that is why it is worse, and I want both of those said in the same breath in a yard and I want them said by somebody who is not me.* **The cost: his name in a printed book for the rest of his life, and the end of his inquiry, because a man who has asked a department to publish his name as a guarantee is not aggrieved, and because he will not be conducting on that reach again although he has been on it every day since the fourth of October.** He also took the count by hand on the sixth at the place where a car comes out of a run and nobody is in the box, and did not write it down, *because a thing I write down is a thing I am expected to be there for next time.* He laid the one-paragraph amendment on the Friday and **put the name on the diversion panel** and was asked twice by men in his own division not to.


### Jo Lask — Volume 02 Batch 0001

- **Where he is:** the round, and a shed, and a corridor. A conductor of thirty, sixteen years' worth of a rule book with his name in it from the third of December, **his hearing still not set and not set on the sixth of December either, and he has stopped checking.**
- **What he did, and it is the reversal and it is his.** He came to a yard at about seven on the Sunday the twenty-first because he could not sit still, said in nine seconds what a tram is — **a sequence with somebody on it, and the list is no use whatever on its own, and what holds it is a person saying the next one out loud** — and then said the sentence the batch is built on: *Nothing is holding it out of here. It is being out of here… The thing that is keeping your car out of this city is the same thing that is keeping the coffee smell on that Row. The length is different, and different is a kind of place, and your car is safe for as long as nobody makes it the same.* He also said the four things you can do and the one you cannot: **you cannot stop a sequence and you cannot seal a length, because there is no edge on it to seal and there is no door, and if you put a thing across it you have made a pinned thing with thirty-three people in it.** And he noticed, before anybody said it, that **the district's own printed rule is on the reach and the length is not the reach, and somebody in that authority could put a box anywhere in the network that is not the reach and would not have broken a single rule to do it.**
- **What he refused.** He **did not go onto the length**, twice asked, and gave the same answer both times: *because it is not my turn and it has never been my turn… if I go out onto that length then in about four months somebody writes down that a conductor of the authority attended, and then in about four years the whole thing is a thing a department did, and the gang is a man in a coat.* He **walked the length on his own time, from the water stairs end to the north end, and was not a party to anything and had not been asked**, and from the north end, at about ten past twelve, in a voice that carried six hundred yards and was not loud, said: **That is us. That is us and not a tram and it is the same noise.** He said beforehand he would be asked whether the whole thing was the best day's work the district has ever done, and that he would say no in about four words, and nobody asked him.

## Tarin Kest

- **Where he is:** a desk in Crown Heights. **He has not come back to the district, has not changed his paragraph, and is not a friend, and he is not the reporting officer and has not been asked.** The department noted, among the matters considered in refusing his variation on the twenty-ninth of November, that a Bench minute permitted one named person to walk a threshold on four dates and that **no similar arrangement is proposed after the fourteenth** — and **his application was produced voluntarily inside two hours of the public safety appeal being lodged, and read out twice by Advocate Quill on her own initiative**, because the day it ambushes you is the day it is theirs. **He has not matched the strings. Soren Rook is not named anywhere in Chapters 1–60.**


### Tarin Kest — Volume 02 Batch 0001

- **He does not appear in Chapters 61–70 and is not named in them.** He has not matched the strings and has not come back to the district, and nothing in this batch changes either of those things, and the three references in the clue chain are references and not pairs.

## The Recovery officer

- **What she is:** a civil servant of nineteen years, and the person in this case who could be dismissed over a number.
- **What closed, and what it cost, on the fifteenth of December:** the department accepted a **certified head count of two hundred and fifty-two** in place of a schedule of rooms, on Advocate Quill's certificate, the per-head schedule was adjusted from two hundred and thirty-one, and **the twenty-one short was recovered.** The thirty-three rooms stay on no list. **No room in this district is on a rate book today because of anything that happened today.** The two hundred and fifty-two figure is now on a per-head schedule and a man who let four rooms behind a boatyard and paid nobody anything for nine years is still on no list at all, and that has not been fixed and was not fixed by anybody.
- **What she did not get:** a rateable basis, and she said in November that she would write to a rates authority and say so, and that it was the first time in nineteen years she had written a letter saying she could not do her job.
- **What she still holds in her own hand and has not given away:** four lines stating that a man of forty-one does not exist, so that the next time his name comes through the counter she can say truthfully that she has never heard of him.


### The Recovery officer — Volume 02 Batch 0001

- **She does not appear in Chapters 61–70 and is not named in them**, and the Volume 01 position is untouched: two hundred and fifty-two on a per-head schedule, the twenty-one short recovered, the thirty-three rooms still on no list, **a man of forty-one still on no list at all, and no room in this district on a rate book today because of anything that happened in December.** The only thing this batch adds is the reason the same trap comes round again from a different direction: **a name in the Atlas is entered against a rateable place, and a maintenance length is not a building and cannot be given a street, and so the passage of the twenty-second of December cannot be recorded at all** — which is the fifteenth of December's own trap, arriving from the tram authority rather than from a continuity order.

## The woman of fifty-four who mends other people's clothes

- **Alive, unnamed on the page, and was told, on Sunday the fourteenth of December, by Lena Marr, in her own front room, where her husband is buried.** Six words off the disposal authority's return: a ground with a name and a plot with a number, and a register for that district that was not kept after the closure. **Lena did not make her ask a question, did not make her ask where to go, and did not say sorry.**
- **What she said, and it is the only thing she says in the volume:** *Nobody has brought me anything in six years. Not even a bill. I have had a fortnight's relief in the first year because I applied for it and I did not know what I was applying for and a woman in an office said it could not be done and gave me the money anyway and would not look at me.*
- **The death record is open. The next-of-kin line is no longer blank and was filled from a card index in four seconds. The cause is on the card. She is not named, was not named in this batch, and is not going to be.**


### The woman of fifty-four who mends other people's clothes — Volume 02 Batch 0001

- **She does not appear in Chapters 61–70 and is not named in them.** She is alive, she is unnamed on the page, she has a plot number in her own hand on a ground whose register was not kept, and she has never had a bill in six years. Her husband is Jonas Venn and his cause of death is in the reopened record and nothing in this batch touches any of it.

## Old Mabb

- **Still asleep on the third floor of Cedar Court since the night of the closure six years ago, awake on the sixth of Sazur and not since, not named anywhere in Chapters 51–60, not written down, and not woken.** On the fifteenth of December, at about twenty-five past eleven in the morning, in the Hall of Wards, in front of about thirty people, **a clerk of the Recovery office read the register out correctly in about four seconds and the answer was a floor and a file number, and nobody in the room said the name, nobody woke him, and no clerk noticed anything and said nothing.**
- **It is on the page that no clerk noticed anything, that a clerk of the Recovery office read a file number out at four seconds' notice and went back to his counter, and that this was the correct conduct of about four officials and has been recorded as such by one of them.** The consequence of the fact is the whole of Chapter 59: the form cannot be completed on that line, and so the rate is stood at by somebody else, and it is a woman of about thirty-four.


### Old Mabb — Volume 02 Batch 0001

- **Still asleep on the third floor of Cedar Court since the night of the closure six years ago, and not named, not written down, and not woken in Chapters 61–70.** He is one line in Mara Quill's flat inventory of the district on the nineteenth of December — *a man asleep on the third floor of a building with no number, who is not written down, and whom nobody has woken* — and nothing else, and **that line is a separate line from Owen Fitch's and always was: the batch's first draft had welded the two into one man, given him Owen's fifth copy and Owen's floor, and CR-030 records the split.** No clerk reads a register out in these ten chapters and no clerk notices anything and no character in this batch is a clerk who noticed anything.

## The woman who keeps a list in Wragg Street

- **Unchanged and still untouched.** Nineteen years, three rooms over a chandler's, a fourth column with a tick in every third line in a copying hand, about six seen in four streets in four months, a husband dead four years, and a woman who cannot say what it is for.
- **What was deliberately not done with it in this batch:** nobody went back and looked. **It was not joined to the carbon relocation schedule found in the reopened order file, and Milo Rook wrote the reason down twice** — *joining them would be me deciding that they are the same, and a man who decides two things are the same in a book is a man who has started making the case instead of the record.* **It does not touch the Quieting, the First Atlas, or the One Survey.**


### The woman who keeps a list in Wragg Street — Volume 02 Batch 0001

- **Unchanged and still untouched.** Nineteen years, three rooms over a chandler's, a fourth column with a tick in every third line in a copying hand, about six seen in four streets in four months, a husband dead four years. **She does not appear in Chapters 61–70 and her name is not written once in them and the tick is not mentioned.** The Volume 1 ruling stands and this batch does not touch it: **the tick was not joined to the carbon relocation schedule, the copied turn sheet, or the new routing slip, and Milo Rook wrote the reason down twice in this batch as well — a column is a case, and he has made one twice in eleven weeks and both times the column made itself into a case.** She is the reason Doreen Sankey refuses to write a man's route home and the reason Pell Harrow's mark is not a job.

## Dilley Farr

- **Where he is:** the route room of the Civic Atlas Office, thirty years, and the man who has signed no deletion in six weeks.
- **What he said, and it is the volume's quietest verdict on the protagonist:** *I have watched three men in this room lose a route in eleven years. Two of them had a wife. Neither of them wrote it down, and both of them stopped coming in, and the department let them go and the department was right to, because a surveyor who cannot find a door is not a surveyor. And you have written yours down, in your own hand, with a date, and you have written the rule at the foot of somebody else's sheet and you have given the thing away in a porch in front of thirty people, and I want you to know that I have signed no deletion in this room for six weeks and the reason is not principle, it is that there has been nothing in front of me worth signing a deletion for. Because of you.* And: *Go and finish your page. I will not read it and I will not be asked, and if anybody in this building asks me what is in it I will say that it is a piece of paper, because there is a woman on a counter in your district who has been saying that to a badge for six weeks and it has begun.*


### Dilley Farr — Volume 02 Batch 0001

- **Where he is:** the route room, thirty-one years, **and he has signed no deletion in this room for about two months.**
- **What he did, and it is the batch's method change in a working man's mouth.** He refused the grammar of the second of four things Milo had written — *you have written *the paper is public and has been for nine weeks* as though that were a thing nobody did. Somebody filled in a box.* — and then said: **There is no such thing as a map that is out of date. There is a map, and there is a list, and the list gets changed by somebody with a form, and after that the map is right and the ground is wrong. I have watched it four times in thirty years in this city and twice in another one. It takes an afternoon. Nobody has to be in a room and nobody has to want anything, and the people who did it are not lying to you — they believe the list, because the list is the department.** And then, with his back to the three atlases: **look for the work, not the road. If a thing in this city has been kept in one piece for a hundred and forty years it is because about nine men were paid to walk it, and it will come apart in a week the day the pay stops, and no amount of looking under a map will tell you anything about it, because the map is the part that gets reprinted.** And on the Friday the twenty-sixth he signed for a second tin on the bottom shelf of his own plan room without asking what was in it, read the lid twice, and said: **that is two copies of a record of work, and two copies is not a record, and a record is a thing a department can produce against you, and there is no department in this city that is going to be stopped by two tins.**

## Halden Vey

- **Named once in the whole case, in a signature block, on one page of a letter from the office of the Prefect dated Thursday the eleventh of December, and does not appear in person.** A man in a car collected the letter at a quarter to five and would not come to the yard. He is the institutional antagonist of the middle volumes and is not this volume's.


### Halden Vey — Volume 02 Batch 0001

- **He does not appear in Chapters 61–70 and his name is not written once in them.** His name is on one page of a letter from the office of the Prefect dated the eleventh of December and Volume 02 does not repeat it. **The office of the Prefect is named twice in this batch, in an instrument: as the authority on the works' notice of the sixteenth of November, and as the authority on service amendment 12/44 — the same standing instruction, ref. 11/D/6, on two documents nine weeks apart, and on a third that does not give the number. That is the whole of the antagonist ladder's second rung as it appears in Volume 02: a reference number, and three documents.**

## Volume 02 new cast, registered in `bible/terminology.md`

- **Nessa Bray** — Line 3 conductor, thirty-eight, sixteen years on the run. **On car 214 from 07:52 on Tuesday the sixteenth of December to 16.41 on Monday the twenty-second: five days and two hours.** She said the next one out loud every single time and she is the reason the car is a car, and she filled in the *Alighted* column herself, and she asked for the shed to be shut, the carbon to stay in the drawer, and the names not to be read out, and got all three. **She is not a device and she is not a symbol and she has said she is going to be asked by nine people whether it was the best day's work this district has ever done and she is going to say no.**
- **Corin Mabey** — Line 3 driver, fifty-one, twenty-nine years. He was on the car and came back with it and **has not been able to say a sentence since the Friday and is at home and is not hurt.**
- **Hester Coombe** — depot clerk, Alder Quay, fifty-one, twenty-two years. **She counts fares, she goes down a carbon with a clerk's pencil, she gave the sheet to two men from a Council transport scrutiny panel out of her own second drawer while she had a witness in the room, and she has not got a second copy and has said so.**
- **Percy Doak** — line controller, thirty-four. **He pulled the screen back to the eighth of October unasked and it took him ninety seconds, and he said *there she is* about a car that had been gone for six days, and he was not being sarcastic.**
- **Roy Pryce** — quay section gang foreman, fifty-four, thirty-one years, on four lengths. **He read a manifest down a telephone and understood a blank line at the bottom of it, and that is why a gang of eleven men walked a length a second time on the twenty-second of December.** He wrote his reason in the hours column in nine words. He will not write a word about a person on that form and he is right and he has said he is prepared to be asked about it in about four months.
- **Cyril Sale** — works clerk, quay section, forty-six, four years in the job; his predecessor was a woman called Mrs Ivell for twenty-two. **He sent a maintenance schedule down for index because a man asked him for it on a telephone; he wrote the margin note in November that made the amendment look self-justifying; and he is the man who said *a road is not a line* and *there is no form*.** He is not a villain and has asked for a week.
- **Amos Trench** — index clerk, the Survey School's repository beneath Crown Heights, sixty-three, forty-one years at the desk, in a coat. **He refused Milo Rook correctly twice, was right both times, and his rule — a school must not hold a copy of another body's maintenance record, because a copy of a record that is true by having been done is a claim that it was done somewhere else — is one of the two halves of the batch's method.**
- **Bettina Sallow** — reader, fifty-four, four years on a hand index at the ninth desk on the left in that room. **She wrote down what Milo Rook said in a room of nine witnesses because that is her work, and she asked him one question, and on the stairs she gave him the thing he needed: eleven route origin sheets in the withdrawal file, nine of them in the building, two not, and an index is a description of what is here and not of what is not here.**
- **Ivy Marsden** — seventy-four, thirty-one years with a market scale at the north end of Saltmarket Row. **She weighs anything, charges nothing, writes nothing down, has eleven sets of brass weights on a board with no name on it, and will not sell the scale and cannot say why.** She watched a man not put a weight on it and said one word: *Right*.
- **Maggie Nye** — sixty-six. **Her sister's son and her nephew were both on car 214 and she wanted his name on a sheet in a yard and could not have it, and was given a sheet of her own, and wrote both names on it in her own kitchen with a date, and put it on a fridge under a magnet off a calendar from a shop on Bridge Street.** She is the only person in the city holding a record of the car, and it is the worst record in the city and it is the only one.
- **Paul Nye**, thirty-eight, and **Ellis Nye**, eleven — the school route, both on the manifest, both alighted, both on the sheet on the fridge.
- **Fenwick** — the yard man with the flag, sixty-three, **who has never looked inside a car in twenty-two years and was not asked to and is not going to be**, and whose book says 16.41 and not 16.36.
- **The man of about fifty in a works jacket**, with a badge that says a number and not a name, on the quay three times in December. **Unidentified, unresolved, and he must not become a face.** A man who takes a clipboard off a wall in the rain and puts it back exactly is not a thief; a courier who has been looking for a man in a list for six years says so and is probably right.
- **The man of about twenty-six in the quay gang**, who stops in the down loop to look at a drain every Monday and Thursday and has done for two years, and whom nobody has ever asked.
- **Rudd** — a clerk of about thirty in the Registry who makes Advocate Quill's requests because she is not permitted to make her own, writes everything down, and asked no questions on the Friday the nineteenth and is glad and would have told her.
- **The man of about forty-five in Survey Enforcement** who walked past a records alcove on the nineteenth, looked at a page, and kept walking. **Unnamed, unremembered, and it is on the page.**

## Ada Fenn, in one line, for the record

**She did not appear in Volume 1 at all and she did not appear in Volume 02 Batch 0001 at all. She was never named in Volume 1 and she is not named in Chapters 61–70. She is referred to in five places across four chapters and in seven age tags, all of them *a woman of sixty-eight* or *a name of sixty-eight*, and one of them is the sentence that says she was not there; two of the seven are inside Milo's own Field Book, which is on the page and is not a reason to strike them out. The eighth sixty-eight tag in the volume, in Chapter 57, is the woman on the fourth floor and is not Ada Fenn, who is on the third floor. Nobody went up three flights of stairs to ask her anything, in any chapter of Volume 01 or Volume 02, and the batch that had her most at stake is the batch in which somebody in a yard asked for her and was refused in four words and then in sixty.** In Chapters 61–70 she is referred to **three times across three chapters, never by name and always as a woman of sixty-eight** — once in a working man's mouth, once in a flat inventory of the district, and once inside Milo's own Field Book — and **a Volume 2 batch may add a reference but must say that it is adding one, and no batch may name her, thank her, argue her back, put her on anything, or send anybody up three flights of stairs.**

---

## Volume 02, Batch 0002 (Chapters 71–80) — state after Chapter 80

**Where everyone is on Wednesday the twenty-first of January.** The hearing has been held and adjourned to the fourteenth of April. The order is suspended and not discharged. Twenty-six names are in a file. A page with a woman's name struck through it is on a bottom shelf in a plan room. An annual return with a box about persons will be filled in again in January. Eleven weather-driven seam events are forecast for a season that begins in five days and cannot be entered in any register. **And nobody in this district has asked Milo Rook for anything for about a fortnight, and he has filled the gap himself, and he has written that down and has decided not to write down what it is.**

### Milo Rook

- **Where he is:** the route room, the second of the two tables under the north window, at about ten past four on the afternoon of Wednesday the twenty-first of January. **Next thing in the book: at a north end at ten to seven tomorrow morning with a chain he will not use, and at a counter on the second of February.**
- **Stage: Stage 2, begun and not complete.** He has identified the dominant grammar of the back length and of Saltmarket Row in Batch 0001. **In this batch he stabilises one thing once, for about ninety seconds, on a wall, on the afternoon of Wednesday the fourteenth — a market, not a wound, about the size of a smell — and he cannot tell whether his stopping and a man's hand coming off the stone were a quarter of a second apart or four minutes apart, because he was not counting, because there is nothing in the world to count with on a wall at half past five. He has decided not to decide for about a fortnight whether he did anything.** He did not enter anything, did not put a weight on a scale, and **is not cured and did not finish the transition.**
- **Crossings: nine. He crossed nothing in Chapters 71–80, at his own decision, in public, in his own book: *I did not cross and I am not going to, and the yard is not asking me to, and the tally has nine lines on it.*** Iona Sen's tally is unchanged at nine lines and nobody has asked him to add one.
- **The orientation ledger, and it did not move.** **The remembered-route list stands at TEN, the original eight intact, none removed, all ten marked by somebody who is not him, and it has not moved since the ninth of December.** The ninth crossing's failure to cost a route is still unexplained and **was not explained by anything in this batch.** The written line home **is the fifty-ninth day of it on the twenty-first of January, is blank, has three marks in three hands, and nobody has asked anybody to fill it in since the twenty-seventh of December** — and he has read back the imperative half of his one sentence in the imperative twice this month and it has not worked, and he has written that down too.
- **The Stage 2 cost, its second instance, unchanged in kind.** A tram gong behind the cold store at about half past four on the fourteenth of January, eleven seconds, **and at four seconds into it he could not tell what it was, and he needed to.** It is a sound and a taste, and no face and no place and no person and no name, and he has still decided nothing about whether that is one thing or two. **The first personal blank, the seam-scar, the record blank and the authorial blank appear nowhere in Chapters 71–80 and may not.**
- **His failure in this batch, and it is the worst thing he has done in four months.** On the morning of Saturday the tenth he wrote a maintenance form for the back length with the gang's six columns and **a seventh he wrote first, in four seconds, headed *If any of them stops*.** Six lines about six people. **He wrote a woman of seventy-four's name and her thirty-one years and put a single pencil line through the whole of it, undated and unreasoned, and folded the page in three and put it in his pocket, and nobody saw him do it.** It went into the second tin on a bottom shelf in his own building at about a quarter past nine that evening, because a foreman's rule in grease pencil about not taking a line off applies to it, and because Dilley Farr would not sign a deletion and would not read it. **A woman's name is legible in a building in Alderquay and Ivy Marsden does not know it and is not going to be told.**
- **What the recovery he got was, and it was not his.** A column headed *wrong* on a sheet of his, a rule at the foot in Rue's hand, and a first correction entered by a man of about twenty-six with a date on the strike and **no name or initial on it, which he has said he will not give.** The rule at the foot: *if a line is wrong, cross it out, put the date on the strike, and put your own initials beside it. Do not tell anybody. Do not take a line off. Do not ask anybody to start again.* **He drew the frame of the map and the frame of the form and the column and the rule at the foot of the first stable field map, and in this batch he wrote a form about people and gave a sheet of his to somebody else, and the sheet is the one that matters.**
- **What he refused.** To put a reference on a gang's schedule. To put a length on a public map. To write a name or an initial on a correction. To be the reason the boundary is checked. To ask the depot clerk to look up the run number before Friday, and then not to chase her about it. To write a cross-reference, a public entry, or a second filing location for a length, and he was refused correctly three times by other people and he agrees with all three.
- **The Lark token: cold throughout, and not out of the coat, and it is the forty-fifth day since the seventh of December at the end of this batch — twenty-five days is the first of January and forty-five is the twenty-first, and the two must not be confused.** It was not warm once in this batch and produced nothing.
- **The Field Book: TEN dated pairs now — one six, four threes, one nine, one two, one five, one eight, one twenty-five** — plus the single-day interval from the tenth of December, still looked at and still not written. **The twentieth of December to the fourteenth of January is twenty-five days, the largest interval in the book and the first one he had a reason to notice, and he joins none of them to any other and does not write down what he thinks ten of a thing means.** Atlas residue used **once in ten chapters, in Chapter 77, in eight words: *A length is missed before it is lost.***
- **The clue chain: not touched.** Nothing in Chapters 71–80 joins any document to any other, and nothing in it is advanced. The second tin is not a fourth instance. **Soren Rook is not named anywhere in Chapters 1–80.**
- **The Lark Street grievance is not named in Chapters 71–80 and is not resolved and is not softened.** It was named once in December and refused once in December and it stands exactly where it stood.

### Mara Quill

- **Where she is:** the Registry, and a front room, with a two-year note, an appeal in one line not stayed, no preferred access, **a custody register page in a drawer in her flat with a coat in it, a minuted refusal to identify a document, twenty-six names in a Crown Heights file that she put there on purpose, and a nine-minute letter to a company secretary dated the sixteenth of January with her name at the bottom of it in biro.**
- **What the custody register page did in this batch: nothing, and she was made to say so in a room.** On the seventh of January, asked by a chair whether she holds a document connecting 11/D/6 to the order, **she took about eleven seconds, looked at the chairman and then at the table, and said: *I hold a document that I am not permitted to produce, I am not going to say what it is, and I would like it recorded that I have declined to identify a document in my custody.*** She did not name it, did not say what it is, did not admit taking it, and was not asked. The chair declined to act on the statement because it is a refusal and not an assertion. **Cass Ardery did not ask, at volume, in a corridor, with a clerk in earshot, and said he would not ask in February and that he had decided it on purpose. Milo Rook did not know what she refused and does not know yet.**
- **What she paid, and she said the price out loud before she paid it.** On Monday the twelfth of January, in the Hall of Wards, about forty people: she read the five conditions on the face of her four-page return document into the record in order, and then said, in about eleven words and at volume: **Twenty-six names go into a file.** *I have told about nine of them that their names are not in any schedule, return, roster, roll, index or register, and one of those nine is a woman of sixty who has been off every list for nine years, and I am about to make that untrue in one line of a minute, and I am doing it because the alternative is that there is no hearing at all, and I would like it recorded that I did it knowing.* Nobody said anything and about nine of forty people looked at each other. **The document was promised not to be entered in any schedule, return, roster, roll, index or register, and it now is, and she has not retracted the promise and has not pretended it still stands.**
- **What she bought with it, and it is permanent while it holds.** The reporting officer's ruling, in four lines, that **no schedule of persons resident at the withdrawn alignment is to be obtained from the district by this department, in this proceeding or in any proceeding, while the answer of the fourth of December stands** — a line with an officer's name on it and not a department on it, and the officer said so on the record and said he could deliver it for about two years.
- **What she did on the sixteenth of January, in nine minutes.** A letter to the company secretary of the tram authority asking that the quay section's annual maintenance return be produced to the reporting officer at the adjourned hearing **in original and not as an extract**, beginning *I have no right to the document I ask for and I do not assert one*, and ending with her name and her own date and a two-year note on her conduct on the face of it. **In a corridor afterwards she said the rest of it is about a piece of paper in a drawer in her own flat, that it has nothing to do with an annual return, that she has not said it and is not going to, and that she has written down in advance that she was not going to and will read it back to herself on the day.**
- **The return route, and the holder, and this is the milestone moving without any warmth in it.** A foreman of fifty-four wrote seven lines in grease pencil in the corner of the card on Sunday the eighteenth — the seventeenth is a Saturday in this batch's chain and the document is dated by the man who wrote it *18.i.* — and it went to **version two**, and for the first time **three of the four copies say version one and one says version two.** **Since Monday the nineteenth the holder is Hettie Brann, and the rule at the foot of the envelope says in her hand: the holder is not M.Q. and is not M.R. and will not be.** Mara Quill did not hand it over kindly, did not explain herself, and **is not going to be the person who maintains it alone either.**
- **What she refused in this batch.** To identify a document in her custody. To speak for a household, which is Lena Marr's job and which she said so. To be named on Cass Ardery's asking. To let her return-route copies be checked by either of the two people who made them. To have her position in the queue at the water clinic counter on the nineteenth put in front of anybody.
- **Nothing romantic has happened and nothing is going to. No kiss, no declaration, no jealousy scene, no rescue. The Lark Street business was not raised in these ten chapters, and she neither raised it nor softened it, and he did not either.** What exists between them in this batch is procedural: a document she refused to identify, a document he did not know she had, and a card with three hands on it that is now held by a woman of sixty-one at a clinic counter.

### Pell Harrow

- **Where he is:** the round, out at eight and in at four, in the district again. Seventeen. **A courier and a Streetkeeper with no power of any kind, and not Milo's assistant.**
- **Tern: still not found, and it is the reason for the blank card.** He went up the south on **Sunday the twenty-eighth of December** at about half past six in the morning, on a coach from the Terrace, with a card and a bag and a coat that was not his, and did not tell anybody he was going and did not tell anybody he would be back. He came back on **Monday the nineteenth of January — twenty-two days — on his own money**, did not telephone, was on the round at eight, and **said nothing about where he had been, and about four people found out from Rue and nobody asked him.** **The card is blank: a courier's card about two inches by three, a line ruled across the middle, a date in his own hand at the bottom, and nothing on either side of the line.** It went face up under the brick. **No chapter in this batch rescued anybody and nothing in it found Tern; the list in the tin is not a finding and Ostrow confirms nothing.**
- **What he did, and it is the sharpest sentence he has ever said to Rue and he did not say it out loud:** *He is back and he is on the round and he is not going to say anything about it and nobody is going to ask him, and I would like that written down by somebody who is not me, and I know who is going to read this in a year and it is not a person in this yard.*
- **His round is shorter and it costs more.** One man short took twenty-two minutes longer on the Thursday the eighth, and from the Monday the fifth the round goes **through the quay section gate on Mondays and Thursdays only**, four minutes longer each way, unpaid, not on a schedule and not asked for. **The second cost of the return-route card is his round: if the holder is ill, the round does it, and Rue said she is going to be leaned on and would rather that was recorded as a cost than as a favour.**
- **The man in the works jacket, and the standing refusal.** A third sighting — Monday the fifth of January at about ten past eight, forty yards off the north end, in a doorway out of the rain, notebook out, wrote twice, and **not there on the Thursday the eighth.** The card went face up under a brick and **nobody followed him on either day.** Rue: *he is not ours and we are not following him and I have said that twice in three months and I would rather be wrong on a Tuesday than right on a Sunday.* **Unidentified, unresolved, and he must not become a face, a faction, or an antagonist.**
- **What he still refuses:** to be the reason a document is made; to be on a sheet; to be thanked; to be legible; to follow a stranger; to be leaned on; **and to say anything about where he was.**

### Rue

- **Where she is:** the round, the trestle table, the porch, the north end of the back length, and the yard.
- **What she did in this batch, and each of it is a rule somebody else now has to keep.** **She asked one question of a man of about twenty-six on a Thursday morning and got the only answer in the batch that nobody had asked for — *whether it still talks* — and the reason she got it is that he said *I am saying it to you because you are not going to write it down*, and she said so, and he agreed.** **She changed the round's rule at the foot of the sheet, in her own hand, without asking the surveyor and said so in front of nineteen people: a column headed *wrong*, and *if a line is wrong, cross it out, put the date on the strike, and put your own initials beside it; do not tell anybody; do not take a line off; do not ask anybody to start again; and a line that is not right for two weeks in a row is not a mistake, it is a place that has changed, and it gets written again.*** **She put the quay section gate on the round on Mondays and Thursdays and said the four minutes out loud, and refused to have it made to look like the same fight as a tram amendment.** **She put seven days into the round for the last week of January and said the price out loud, twice, and named the version of it she would rather have got wrong.** And she said the thing about Pell in a yard and asked for it to be written down by somebody who was not her, and nobody wrote it down.
- **What she said that nobody argued with:** *He is not ours and we are not following him.* And: *a tram authority's amendment committee cannot hear anything about a stop on a round, and I am not going to stand in a canteen and ask a man to put my round in a book so that it looks like the same fight. It is a gate that is open twice a week.*
- **What she refused, again:** to write a man's route home; to have the round put on a schedule; to be leaned on; to be thanked; **and to be the one who corrects a sheet of his.** The rule at the foot of the return-route envelope that names her exclusions is **Hettie Brann's** and Rue put nothing on it.

### Hettie Brann

- **Where she is:** the water clinic counter, nineteen years on it. **The tally is her paper and not her apron pocket: it is a clinic counter form in her own hand, on the back of a form, as Volume 01 Chapter 56 has it, and it has been in Dr Sen's apron pocket since the twenty-ninth of December, when she wrote the ninth line on it, turned the tally round and put it away before anybody asked her for it. It has nine lines on it and it is the record of his crossings and Hettie Brann does not read it and has never read it. Chapter 80 states both halves in one sentence, and a later batch may not move the tally from Dr Sen to her and may not put it in Hettie Brann's pocket.**
- **What she did in this batch, and it is the relationship's whole vocabulary in one exchange.** On **Monday the nineteenth of January at about ten past nine in the morning** she was asked whether she would hold four pieces of paper, **and she said yes in about four words and set one condition in about forty, and nobody thanked her and she was not thanked on the Thursday, and she has said she would rather be a nurse.** *I am not doing it out of kindness and I want that said first. I have been asked for a route before by a woman of about thirty-one in a raincoat and I said no, and I was right, and I have not changed my mind about the raincoat… I am taking the envelope because there are four pieces of paper in four buildings and somebody has to know whether they are all saying the same thing.* Her rule, in her own hand, in about eleven seconds: **first working day of the month — the four copies are taken out of the four places and looked at, the version and the date are compared and nothing else is read; if one is not the same, ask the person whose hand it is and nobody else; the holder is not M.Q. and is not M.R. and will not be.** And her condition on her own illness: **then the round does it**, and I would like that written down as the second cost of this card and not as a favour.
- **The first working day of the month was the first of January and there was no holder then, and nobody knows whether the four copies were the same.** The next three are the second of February, the second of March and the first of April.
- **What she has not been asked to be and will not be:** the door-check, the reader, the person who holds a mark overnight, **or the person who knows what is written on four pieces of paper.** *If anybody asks me what is on it I will say it is a piece of paper.*

### Roy Pryce

- **Where he is:** the quay section, four rooms above a fitter's shop, fifty-four, thirty-one years, four lengths.
- **What he did in this batch, and it is three refusals and a sentence.** **He refused a cross-reference on his schedule, at the wall, in the cold, in about nine words, on the ground that a row with two numbers on it is a row somebody asks about in four years and *maintenance length* is a phrase that means *not a place* for about four years and then means *not yet a place*.** He told Milo Rook to **ask him again in about four months, when he knows what a fella in an office is going to say to him, and not now, because now it is a Thursday practice and in four months it is a document.** He gave the batch's answer about records in a hut with a kettle on, standing up, in about ninety words: **the sheet is the gang's, it is not a record of the length, and so where is the length's record — there is no such thing; there is *I am the record*, eleven men twice a week since the year before the war, and if I stop coming the length is not kept and it is a piece of track with a fence round it.** He put the round through the quay section gate on Mondays and Thursdays on the condition that there is a lock on the hut and the key is on a nail and eleven men need it. **On Sunday the eighteenth of January he wrote seven lines in grease pencil in the corner of a card about a gate, a padlock, and the fact that there is nowhere to turn in that compound, and ended it: *This is not a route and I am not redrawing anybody's route.*** He signed the annual return on the Wednesday morning with the clerk's sixth line not read out to him. **The reason column on the gang's sheet is empty on all six of the walks in Chapters 71–80 and that is correct, and he has still not written one word about a person on a form, and in about four months a man in an office is going to ask him what he saw.**
- **What he still refuses:** a reference on his schedule; a name in any box; being the officer who answers for persons; and **being redrawn by a surveyor.**

### Cyril Sale

- **Where he is:** the quay section office, forty-six, four years in the job, his predecessor a woman called Mrs Ivell for twenty-two years. **He is not a villain and has not become one.**
- **What he did in this batch, and it is the whole of the batch's institutional argument in a working man's hands.** He **refused a cross-reference and said why: a reference is a place other people can write into, and not one of the nine people who can find his four numbers has a shovel, and *nil* in a rateable column is a hole with a sign over it, and the rack will be cleared in about eleven years and no piece of paper will stop it and the thing on Milo's paper will make it worse.** He **filled the fifth box of the annual return — *persons observed on the length during the year* — with nil**, after standing at a window for four minutes, **and gave the reason out loud in a yard, which is that if he writes persons in there then next January there is a return in a file that says persons were observed on the quay service lengths, and the only name available to be asked is the foreman's, and there is no column on that form for the answer.** *A nil on a length is not a nil on a street, and the reason it is not is that there is nobody on a length to be observed and there is a gang on it, and those are two different facts and they are in the same box.* **He beat Milo Rook's counter-argument in about nine words — the crew was not observed, the crew was the observer — and Milo Rook has written that down as the worst moment of his month.** And on the Wednesday the fourteenth he **filled the sixth line in fifty-four words in his own hand, naming himself, and it is the only true sentence anybody in Alderquay has written about the thirteenth of October: the length was added at the fourth edition in November per amendment 12/44 under 11/D/6, the margin note was his, the third edition did not contain the length, and the amendment's lines for requested by and instructing officer are blank and he cannot supply either.** He carried the form out of his own office at four o'clock and put it on a table in a yard because the yard is where people are, and took it back at half past four, and **let nobody copy it, and about four people have said since that a man who could have copied it and did not is the reason this district still does not have a copy of a form with a box on it about persons.** **There is no copy. The district has no standing. Milo Rook does not know and will not find out in this volume.** *That is where a true sentence goes. Everywhere else it is a speech.*

### Lena Marr

- **Where she is:** the river core, the Board school, a tram authority's committee room above the Parcel Street canteen, and a yard. **About thirty-four. Two children of her own. Not on the rate roll and never has been. The named rateable head of a temporary accommodation household for two hundred and fifty-two people in a building with no number, at two and four a head a week, in advance, week by week, with no term.**
- **What she did in this batch, and it is the district's standing position in one paragraph.** On **Wednesday the seventh of January at about eleven o'clock in the morning, in a room above a canteen, as the only person in eleven streets who could be asked the question under rule 14 of the tram authority's amendment procedure** — which is that a party must show an effect upon a person, and that an effect is a change in access to a route, a service, a rate, a residence, a burial place or a school place — **she answered: *There is no effect on me. That is the answer… The thirteenth of October is what keeps me whole.*** *Two and four a head a week, in advance, week by week, with no term, charged to my name and not to the district, for every person entered in a building that has no street. And that is what being unaffected is. If the district had produced a rateable basis for that block in October then the compensation would have stopped, and the seven children would have lost the place the intake is going to give them in the spring, and I would have lost the only piece of paper in this city that says two hundred and fifty-two people are in those rooms.* When the chair asked whether her attendance was a decision or a thing she had come to say, she said it was a thing she had come to say, **and asked it recorded that she had been asked by a member of a tram authority's committee at about eleven o'clock in the morning on the seventh of January whether she was content, and that she was not being bitter, because she has been told twice this month that she sounds bitter, and that she had not come to argue with anybody.**
- **Unchanged and paid:** the levy, the seven school places, and the two children lost in October who are still lost and about whom no batch has said the word back since the fifteenth of December. **Nothing in Chapters 71–80 touches either.**
- **What she still refuses:** to be the one who chooses what the district gets; to be a party; to apologise to anybody for a department; to put a name on a district sheet; **and to be content.**

### Doreen Sankey

- **Where he is:** two rooms behind a boatyard on the Ferry Road side, forty-one, on no rate book, no index, no tenancy and no schedule, and never has been, and still would rather be hard to find. **He does not appear in Chapters 71–80 and is not named in them, and that is structural: the batch's labour is other people's and he is not asked to carry it.**
- **What is unchanged and binding:** he is still the only person in this district who can turn round in the Ferry Road side and come back, he does it eleven times a day, and **he was not asked to walk anybody in this batch and did not offer.** The man of about twenty-six on the quay gang is not his to ask about, **and the standing note that nobody in the district has ever asked that man and Doreen Sankey is not not going to start has been broken by Rue, once, in the correct way, and not by him.**

### Tomas Rusk

- **Sixty-one. Still will not swear. Still on a drainage record and a works maintenance schedule and a name on a tram diversion panel, and all of it is his and none of it is a street. He does not appear in Chapters 71–80 and is not named in them.** The chapter the batch built on his sentence, Chapter 72's fourth answer, is his sentence being finished by somebody else at length: *the sheet is not a record of the length; I am the record.* **And the batch's answer to who keeps a length is two jobs done by two sets of people — the writing down and the walking — and he has still not been asked to say it and has not been asked in this batch, and nobody wanted him to.**

### Iona Sen

- **Where she is:** the water clinic, and the dispensary counter. **The water clinic counter runs on the Tuesdays and the Fridays and has since before anybody in this district was born, and she did not choose that and it is in the book.**
- **What she did in this batch, and it is the cost of the month.** On **Tuesday the thirteenth of January at about four o'clock** she read Milo's seventh-column form out loud on her own counter, in a room with nine people in it, at volume, and told the nurse at the back to say why he was in with her, *because if you do not tell them why they will think it is an emergency and it is not an emergency, it is the other thing.* She told the room a document was about six people, that the last line was **a woman of seventy-four whom she has seen twice at that counter and who is not on anything**, that her name was in it, and that there was a line drawn through it with no date and no reason. **And she gave the new shape of the fifth finding, which is worse than the old one: in November you held a page; on Saturday you filled it in. *A thing you have destroyed has not happened and a thing you have hidden has happened twice, and I have watched four people in six years in this district be eaten by a page they could not get rid of and not one of them was in the wrong.*** She also **opened the water clinic every day of the week of Monday the twenty-sixth of January from eight until six, on her own decision, unasked**, and said at the counter: *I am not going to be told by a four-page document from an office that does not sign its name what day somebody in this district is ill. That is a claim on my counter and I have had four claims on my counter in three months and I have refused three of them.* She has said nothing about the Atlas, nothing about her own past, and nothing about the man who taught Milo's hand. **The fifth finding is not cured, is not signed, and cannot be signed.**

### Ola Petrossian

- **Twenty-three, four months in the job, never been past the tram steps. In Chapters 71–80 she is a nurse at the back of the water clinic counter who is told to say out loud why the surveyor is in with the doctor, and who says it.** She has still not walked him to anything, was not asked to, and has still not been thanked, and has still never been thanked for a card with a number on it in December.

### The man of about twenty-six in the quay gang

- **He is not named, has no initial on the page, and has said that if anybody asks him to put one there he will not do it.** Two years of stopping in the down loop to look at a drain, never asked until Thursday the eighth of January at about ten past seven, when a woman with a satchel asked him one question and he answered her and not the surveyor, **because she was the person and he was not.** *Whether it still talks.* **The drain still talks and it is on the page and it is a drain and it is not a seam and it is not a remnant and it is the only instrument in this case that is not a document and not a person.** On the evening of the fourteenth he made the first correction on the district's stable map, in his own hand, with a date on the strike and nothing else, and said he wrote it because the column was there and because a surveyor stood at a doorway for four days with a watch out and did not once ask anybody a question about the wall. **Nobody asked him to write it and nobody thanked him for it.**

### Joan Lask

- **He does not appear in Chapters 71–80 and is not named in them, and his hearing is still not set and he has stopped checking.** His printed rule book and his name in it and the reach and the count are untouched, and nothing in this batch reopens the question he raised on the twenty-first of December, which is that a route stays distinct from the present only while somebody can say the space is clear before the next turn. **The batch's answer to who keeps a length is the same answer in different words, and he did not say it and has not been asked about it.**

### Dilley Farr

- **Where he is:** the route room, thirty-one years, **and he has now signed no deletion in that room for about three months and the reason is still not principle.** The two tins on the bottom shelf: the gang's carbon, four weeks of it, and **one page that is not a gang sheet, which he has not read, and the last line of which is a name with a line through it, and he is not going to know whose name it is and has said that he has never in thirty-one years been glad of a thing in a drawer more than he is going to be glad of that one.** He is the man who signed for the tin and has not been asked to sign for it again, because a foreman wrote the standing instruction on the side of it in grease pencil, and he said in December that the forecasts and the returns of this office cannot be entered anywhere and the council has not asked.
- **What he said in this batch, and each of it is the whole of a discovery:** *There is no such thing as a map that is out of date… a box that wants to know why is the amendment… **a public map is not a place to put a length**… nothing is a very good answer and it is very hard to argue with, and the moment you put nothing in a column it stops being nothing and becomes a hole with a heading on it, and a heading is a thing somebody can look up… I am not going to sign it, and that is not a refusal, and I have signed a great many things in thirty-one years and I have never signed one of those and I am not going to start in January.*** And: *Write your query down again, in your own book, and put on it the thing the man in the drainage section said, in his words, and then do not send it anywhere, because there is nowhere to send it, and the sending is the part that has been going wrong for forty-one years.*

### Cass Ardery

- **Second-in-command on an investigation of a thing he classified. Still not the reporting officer. In Chapters 71–80 he is the man who did not ask, at volume, in a corridor, with a clerk in earshot, and who said he had decided it on purpose.** *I am not asking now and I will not ask in February, and there is no version of this that ends with me asking you in a room in front of a chair. You will want a reason. The reason is that I wrote a classification of a site on the eleventh of the current year, and it was correct on the day I made it, and I have had four months to find out what it is like to hold a piece of paper that somebody else wants, and it is like this.* **He also said in December that the hearing would not be set in January; he was wrong about January, he knows he was wrong about January, and nobody told him so and nobody is going to.** He asked in a hearing chamber, on Monday the twelfth, for the adjournment to be recorded as a departmental list day and not the reporting officer's, **and it is minuted.** His reason for not being the reporting officer is unchanged: there is no rule in this department that says a man who made a mistake cannot report on it, and not one rule that says he can, and that silence is the reason.

### Ivo Sarratt — Volume 02 Batch 0002

- **Reporting officer on the investigation into the district continuity order CO/6/BELL. Survey Enforcement. Fifty-one, twelve years. He is a person other than any person named in the Prefect's letter of the eleventh of December or in the order of six years back, and he was named on the seventh of January after three weeks of the file being passed down, and the notice is on the register of the Hall of Wards.** He asked in writing for the exclusion in writing and for the order of reference on the letter of the eleventh of December, **which is the only document in the whole case that would let him apply to anybody in another department, and which was produced in twenty-two minutes.** He is not a friend and is not an ally. **What he found, in four hours, and none of it is a victory for anybody:** the order stands suspended and is not discharged; the classification of the eleventh **was correct on the day it was made and is not correct now and stays withdrawn**; the removed sentence **cannot be restored by anybody in this city** and the department cannot account for its removal; the district's return document is received with its five conditions read into the record; **and a schedule of persons is not to be obtained from this district, in this proceeding or in any proceeding, while the answer of the fourth of December stands** — a line he wrote with his own name on it and not the department's, and said so on the record, and said: *I have undertaken a thing I can deliver for about two years, and in two years I will either be in a different job or I will not be, and if I am not, this is a line with my name on it in a file and somebody can look at me.* **The matter is adjourned to Tuesday the fourteenth of April and he is adjourned with it, and the date is a departmental list day and not his.**

### Ada Fenn — Volume 02 Batch 0002

- **She does not appear in Chapters 71–80 and her name is not written once in them. She is referred to once, in the established idiom and never by name, as *a woman of sixty-eight on nothing*, in Milo Rook's own Field Book in Chapter 80, inside an inventory of the district's standing prices. That is the fourth reference across four chapters of Volume 02 — Batch 0001 had three, in a working man's mouth, in a flat inventory, and in Milo's own book — and this batch is adding one and says so here, as the state layer requires. Nobody went up three flights of stairs about her. She is not thanked, argued back, put on anything, or solved, and the prohibition remains a standing note and not a problem.** Unchanged and binding: she has refused to interpret the third bell thirteen times and will not be asked a fourteenth; she is not on the rota and will not be.

### Owen Fitch, Old Mabb, the woman of fifty-four, Nell Crale, Verrick Assal, Orrin Sable, Halden Vey, Tarin Kest, Jo Lask, Nessa Bray, Hester Coombe, Ivy Marsden, Doreen Sankey and Tomas Rusk

- **None of them appears in Chapters 71–80 except as recorded above.** **Owen Fitch's name is still on five copies of a sheet of paper with a contractor's head on it, in a space somebody filled in on purpose, with a floor that is not his, and it has not been taken off any of them and no clerk took it off.** **Old Mabb is one clause in Chapter 80's Field Book inventory — a man asleep on a third floor who is not written down and whom nobody has woken — and it is a clause of his own and is not Owen's.** **The woman of fifty-four has her plot number and no bill and is not in these chapters.** **Orrin Sable, Halden Vey, Sel Iri and Tarin Kest do not appear and are not named.** **Nessa Bray, Hester Coombe, Ivy Marsden, Doreen Sankey and Tomas Rusk appear only as recorded in their own entries above.**

### New minor proper nouns registered by this batch, in `bible/terminology.md`

- **The authority's amendment committee** — three members, convened under rule 14, a room above the Parcel Street canteen with a barometer in it, and a minute in four paragraphs that records that it could not identify the person who requested amendment 12/44.
- **Rule 14, paragraph three** — the standing test, quotable in full, and the reason a district cannot be a party.
- **Ivo Sarratt** — reporting officer, Survey Enforcement, fifty-one, twelve years.
- **A clerk of about thirty-four at the district index counter** — eleven years in the section, no name, who said *the book is not a record of what happened, it is a record of what is where*, and who told him to ask the other question before he left.
- **A clerk of about thirty-four in Survey Enforcement** who crossed two names off a list of officers to find a reporting officer, and who has no name and is not a character.
- **A man of about fifty in the drainage section** — nineteen years in the room, no name, who explained that there has never been a district office for Mireward and that a man of fifty-eight goes out in the summer with a rod and has done since 1974.
- **Percy, fifty-eight** — the man who goes out into Mireward on the first dry week and writes four lines on a pad, **mentioned, not present, and he may not be entered as a case or visited.**
- **Rudd** — the Registry clerk of about thirty who makes Advocate Quill's requests because she is not permitted to make her own, and who wrote everything down and asked no questions on the nineteenth of December. **He does not appear in Chapters 71–80 and is not named in them, and nothing in this batch is a second instance of him.** The two clerks of about thirty-four in this batch are a different matter: the district index counter clerk who said *the book is not a record of what happened, it is a record of what is where*, and a Survey Enforcement clerk who crossed two names off a list of officers in order to find a reporting officer. **Neither is named, neither is a character, and a later batch may decide in prose whether either is a person or a post, but the state layer may not merge either of them with Rudd.**
- **The man in the works jacket** — a third sighting on Monday the fifth of January, forty yards off the north end, a doorway out of the rain, notebook out, wrote twice, and not there on the Thursday the eighth. **Unidentified, unresolved, and the standing prohibition that he must not become a face, a faction or an antagonist is registered with him.**- **The annual maintenance return** — a printed single-side form, about four hundred in the authority, one per section per January, with a fifth box headed *persons observed on the length during the year* and a sixth headed *action taken by the officer*.
- **The district returns card** — printed out of a machine, about nine hundred a month, with *Officer responsible: [blank]* and a machine dash against the reason.

---

## Volume 02, Batch 0003 (Chapters 81–90) — state after Chapter 90

**Where everyone is on Friday the twentieth of February.** The first weather-driven seam event of the season happened at twenty past two on the morning of the twenty-ninth of January and the second happened in daylight on the nineteenth of February, and **neither of them is in any file, and one of them is in a printed sheet in a man's own hand and the other one has no row in this city at all.** A surveyor wrote a form for the season and made the case against his own district in a yard. A fishmonger gave a sheet of paper a second side. A man on no list told four people which of four pieces of paper had been read. A woman of sixty on a second landing was told that she is on a list she refused for nine years and that she cannot see it. The second of March is the next first working day of the month. **The fourteenth of April is the last day of the season and the day of the hearing, and nobody in this city has told either office about the other, and the only person who has is a woman of sixty-one at a clinic counter.**

### Milo Rook

- **Where he is:** the route room and the top of a hundred and ten yards of frontage that is not a street. **Next thing in the book: at a counter on the second of March.**
- **Stage: Stage 2, begun and not complete, and he did not hold anything in these ten chapters.** He went in nowhere, crossed nothing, stabilised nothing, and **has decided not to be a man who turns a length into a wound because he has learned a new thing about wounds.** He did identify a fourth thing and could not classify it — a row of frontages that puts fish scales out of a wet stone floor in the rain — **and wrote down that he was not going to invent a category for it, and that inventing one would be the same mistake three times over.**
- **Crossings: nine. He crossed nothing in Chapters 81–90**, and the reason is on the page twice: the second event happened in daylight in front of about nine people and did not need him at all, and *I am not going to be a man who turns a length into a wound.* **Iona Sen's tally is unchanged at nine lines, the paper is Hettie Brann's and the pocket is Dr Sen's, and he has not looked at it since the thirteenth of January.**
- **The orientation ledger, and it did not move. The remembered-route list stands at TEN, the original eight intact, none removed, all ten marked by somebody who is not him, nothing added and nothing lost, and the ninth crossing's failure to cost one is still unexplained and is not explained by anything in these ten chapters.** The written line home **is the eighty-ninth day of it on the twentieth of February, is blank, has three marks in three hands from Batch 0001, and nobody has asked anybody to fill it in since the twenty-seventh of December** — and **the district's not asking is now on the page as a refusal and not as a kindness, in his own hand: *a thing everybody has quietly agreed not to ask about is a thing that has quietly become nobody's, and this district has lost two rooms and a school place and a grave in six years to things nobody asked about.***
- **The Stage 2 cost, its third instance, a taste.** Nine cups out of the same tin and the same pot in one day on the Saturday the thirty-first, and at the ninth he put the cup down, **because he wanted to know whether he had been awake since seven and had used taste as an instrument and it was not there.** He used a watch instead, and has said the watch is the correct procedure and that he does not like it. **A sound and a taste, no face, no place, no person and no name, and he has still decided nothing about whether that is one thing or two. The first personal blank, the seam-scar, the record blank and the authorial blank appear nowhere in Chapters 81–90.**
- **His failure in this batch, and it is the same mistake for the third time in five weeks and it is worse than January's.** A form headed *SEAM EVENTS — RIVER CORE — WEEKLY* with the gang's six columns and a **seventh headed SCHEDULED, which is not a question but a column of days**, filled in over nine minutes for nine days **off a card two inches by three in a frame at the water clinic that says what time the round goes past the door.** **He put another woman's week into a surveyor's form out of a nurse's card.** And then, in a yard, in about eleven seconds, **he made the case for a zero against his own district** — that a district entered as expecting weather-driven seam events is a district that can be asked why it did not have them, and that the only difference between this district and Mireward is that about nine people have been here and nobody has been there — **and it cannot be unsaid, and a doctor said so in front of nineteen people, and about four people noticed that nobody wrote it down.** What he did with the sheet: struck the seventh column, dated the strike, **wrote no reason**, wrote four people's objections on the back in their words, did not consult Rue, said so in front of nineteen people, **and kept it in his own coat**, on the stated ground that there is nowhere in this city to put a bad idea of his that is safer than he is.
- **The recovery he got was somebody else's work, twice, and neither of them is his.** A **second side** on the sheet of who does what and on which day, **headed and ruled in a fishmonger's hand, with a rule at the foot that a side with nothing on it is a day when nobody stood in the weather**, and a first line with a man's initials on it that are new, and the reason for the initials given out loud. And **Doreen Sankey's walk**, done in about an hour on the tide, nobody sent him, which produced the district's first usable fact about four copies of a card in five weeks: **you know a paper has been read by where it is soft.** He wrote down that it does not scale and that he is not going to build a version of it and put a column on a sheet saying somebody should do it, **because the second I do that it is a job, and a job is a thing a company can ask about.**
- **What he refused.** To tear up a bad idea of his. To let Rue correct his own map. To put a line under any of the three cards on the trestle table. To choose between the two things a run number can mean. To write down what he saw at the north end. To say the seventh-column thing in a room again for about a year. To go to Mireward. To be told the fourth copy's whereabouts. **To ask Mara Quill about the seventh of January, and he has written down that not asking was the correct procedure and not a courtesy, and that in December he watched her look at a table for about four seconds and wrote down something else.**
- **The Lark token: cold throughout, not out of the coat, and the figures on the page are the fifty-third day on the twenty-ninth of January, the fifty-fourth on the thirtieth, the fifty-fifth on the thirty-first, the fifty-sixth on the first of February, the fifty-seventh on the second, the sixtieth on the fifth, the sixty-seventh on the twelfth, the seventy-fourth on the nineteenth and the seventy-fifth on the twentieth**, all since the seventh of December. **It was not warm once in this batch and produced nothing**, and in the last entry of the batch he has written that a book that counts a coat is a book that has run out of other things.
- **The Field Book: THIRTEEN dated pairs — one six, four threes, one nine, one two, one five, one eight, one twenty-five, and three added in February which are a three and two sevens** — plus the single-day interval from the tenth of December, still looked at and still not written. **The pair in front of the nineteenth of February is seven days, off the twelfth, and the book has been written nine times since the twenty-ninth of January on eight dates. The February three is a fifth three and is not one of the four, the four threes are not collapsed, the fifteen-day silence from the fourteenth to the twenty-ninth of January was never written down because a silence is not a pair, and he joins none of them to any other and writes down that he does not understand what thirteen of a thing means. There is no thirty-six and no eleventh pair.** Atlas residue used **once in ten chapters, in Chapter 89, in six words: *Weather uses whatever door is open.*** Ten entries in the book in this batch, on nine dates, **and the twenty-first of January has none**, and he has gone back and checked that twice.
- **What he has now written down that he had not, and it is the batch's finding:** **every method this district has for keeping a place — a gang, a round, a rota, a card, an envelope, a tin on a bottom shelf — answers the question it was built to answer and not one of them answers whether a person is on a page. A card tells you which way to turn and not whether there is a way to turn. A sheet tells you who does what on a day and not whether the day was safe. A return tells you what eleven men did and not why. A tin tells you the sheet was not lost and not what is on it.** And the honest answer to *is there an instrument*: **yes, and it is not a document, and it is a person who is on no list, and it does not scale.**
- **The clue chain: not touched and not advanced.** Nothing in Chapters 81–90 joins any document to any other. **The second tin is not a fourth instance. Two documents still carry a pair of marks and not three; three references exist and are references and not pairs; Kest has not matched the strings; and Soren Rook is not named anywhere in Chapters 1–90.** **The volume's final image is not written and no batch may write it without the outline.**
- **The Lark Street grievance is not named in Chapters 81–90, and is not resolved and not softened and not referred to.**
- **How he ends the batch, in his own hand:** *I am not going to be the author of the back length's maintenance, and I have not been for five weeks, and a man of fifty-four walks it twice a week and a woman of about forty-five puts her thumb in a crack twice a day and a man of about sixty puts his hand on a corner for four seconds twice a day and none of them knows me and I have known all three of them for five months and I have not asked one of them for anything, and that is the only thing I have done this winter that I would be willing to write the word method next to.* **He is not cured, and the seventh column is the same mistake every time, and the state layer records that as an open thing and not a closed one.**

### Mara Quill
